From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what brings me in is I’ve been having this problem at work where I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’m an electrician, and I’ll finish something that should be straightforward, like an install or checking a panel, and then I can’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nI’ll check the breakers, the labels, the torque marks, the photos I took—sometimes I take photos just so I can prove to myself I did it right—and then I’ll still go back and look again. There have been times I’ve actually driven back to a site because I couldn’t shake the feeling that I missed something. And it’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I’ve been doing this a long time. But a simple job ends up taking way longer than it should, and lately people are starting to notice. That’s what worries me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having these repetitive thoughts or behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of your work as an electrician?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the work stuff, honestly. That’s where it really shows up and where it’s causing problems.\n\nLike I said, I’ll finish a job and then I just get stuck in this loop of checking the same things—breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took—over and over. And even when I’ve checked it, there’s this nagging “what if I missed something?” feeling, so I’ll go back again. Sometimes I’ve even driven back to a site after leaving, just because I couldn’t settle down about it.\n\nI guess I’m not saying it never happens anywhere else, but the thing that’s really gotten out of hand is at work. A simple install can take way longer than it should, and now people are starting to notice, which is kind of what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking things over and over, is it because you're afraid of something bad happening if you don't get it just right?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. It’s not just, like, “I want it neat” or “I want the label straight.” It’s more that even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect—like I can remember checking it—this thought barges in that maybe I missed one loose neutral, or maybe I left something just slightly wrong that could heat up and start a fire.\n\nAnd I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you checked it, you know what you’re doing,” but it doesn’t really land. The thought just keeps following me. I can be halfway home and suddenly I’m picturing it, like, what if something’s sitting there loose behind the cover and nobody knows until it causes damage. So then I start replaying the whole job in my head, step by step, and it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension or anxiety when you're having these repetitive thoughts about your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I definitely feel worked up in my head when it’s happening, like I can’t settle it, but I haven’t really paid close attention to my body in that moment.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I get some specific physical thing every time, like a particular symptom I could point to. It’s more that I get kind of keyed up and stuck on it, and I’ll feel like I can’t relax until I’ve checked it again or gone back through it in my head. But as far as, like, noticing tension in one place or anything like that… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or have a certain symmetry to them for you to consider them complete?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way, at least. Like, I’m not usually looking for things to be symmetrical or arranged a certain way before I can move on.\n\nWith work, there are obviously steps that have to happen in a certain order because that’s just how the job is done safely, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This has to be perfectly even,” or “I need to do this sequence exactly or it feels wrong.” It’s more practical than that, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself needing symmetry in my house or with objects or anything like that. If something’s crooked, I might straighten it like anyone would, but I don’t feel stuck on it the way I get stuck with checking work stuff. So… no, I don’t think that’s a big thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work isn't complete unless it's perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is close, but for me it’s less like perfect-looking and more like I can’t feel done unless I’ve made absolutely sure I didn’t leave anything unsafe or wrong.\n\nLike, I’ll finish what should be a pretty straightforward install, and then I’m going back over it again and again. I’ll check the breakers, check the labels, look at torque marks, look through the photos I took, sometimes compare them in my head to what I remember doing. And even if everything seems right, I’ll still get this feeling like, “Wait, did I really verify that one?” or “Did I miss something small?” So then I’m back at the panel or staring at the pictures longer than I need to.\n\nAnd it’s gotten to where simple jobs take me way longer than they should. I’m not just doing a normal double-check anymore. I’ll keep circling back, and there have been times I’ve actually driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go. That’s the part that’s really messing with me, because people are starting to notice that I’m taking too long or getting hung up on stuff. So… yeah, I guess it doesn’t feel complete unless it feels completely right, and I have a hard time trusting that it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to control something when you're checking things over and over at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if I would’ve used the word “control” at first, but that’s kind of what it feels like. Like I’m trying to get control over the possibility that I missed something.\n\nAt work, it’s mostly that I keep going back over the same things again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and then sometimes I’ll start thinking, “Wait, did I actually check that, or am I just remembering checking it?” And then I’m back looking again. Even when the job is basically done and nothing looks wrong, I still feel like I can’t quite let it be finished.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick normal check anymore. A simple install can take way longer than it should because I keep circling back over it. I’ve even driven back to a site sometimes because I couldn’t shake the feeling that I needed to verify something one more time. So yeah, I guess I’m trying to control that uncertainty, or control the risk that something was left wrong. But it gets to where the checking is taking over, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of making a mistake that's driving you to check things over and over, or is it more about making sure everything is done to a high standard?\n\nPatient: It’s more the mistake part, I think. I mean, I do care about doing good work — I’ve always taken pride in that — but the thing that gets stuck in my head isn’t, “Is this up to some perfect standard?” It’s more like, “What if I missed something dangerous?”\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought will just barge in on me that maybe there’s one loose neutral I didn’t catch, or maybe I left something in a way that could start a fire. And I can try to tell myself, “No, you checked it, you looked at it, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really settle. It follows me. I’ll be driving home and replaying it in my head, trying to remember exactly what I saw or what my hand did, and then I start doubting whether I actually verified it or just think I did.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not really about making it beautiful or above-and-beyond perfect. It’s the possibility that I made one mistake and that one mistake could matter. That’s what pulls me back into checking.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to check things over and over because you're afraid of the consequences of making a mistake, rather than because you want everything to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s the consequences. I mean, I care about doing things right, obviously, especially with electrical work, but the thing that hooks me isn’t like, “Oh, the work won’t be perfect enough.” It’s more like this thought barges in — even after I already checked a panel or a disconnect — that maybe I missed one loose neutral, or maybe I left something in there that could overheat or start a fire.\n\nAnd I’ll try to shake it off. I’ll tell myself, “You checked it, you know you checked it,” but then my brain starts picking at it, like, “Are you sure? What if you only think you checked it?” And that feeling doesn’t just stay at the job. It follows me all the way home sometimes. I’ll be driving and still thinking about that one thing, replaying it, trying to convince myself I didn’t miss anything dangerous.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not really about perfect. It’s fear that one mistake could have serious consequences, and I can’t seem to let that possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check at work need to be in a specific order or have a certain exactness to them for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: It’s not so much that it has to be in one exact order, like a ritualized sequence or anything. I mean, I do have a way I normally work through things because that’s just part of doing the job safely, but the problem is more that I keep not trusting that I’m done.\n\nSo I’ll check the breakers, then I’ll check the labels, then I’ll look at the torque marks, and then I’ll look at the photos I took. And then instead of moving on, I’ll start over in my head, like, “Okay, but did I actually verify that breaker? Did I match that label right? Did I really see the torque mark, or am I just assuming I did?” And then I’m back there checking again.\n\nSometimes it turns into this loop where I can’t leave it alone. A simple install that should be pretty straightforward ends up taking way longer than it should because I keep going back over the same pieces. And even if I do leave, sometimes I’ll still be thinking about it enough that I’ve driven back to a site just to look at something one more time.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s exactness in the sense of needing it to be arranged perfectly. It’s more that I need to feel certain I didn’t miss something, and I keep checking until I can almost convince myself it’s okay to move on. But lately that point takes a long time to get to, and people are starting to notice I’m getting stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things over and over if there were no potential consequences for making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, if there were really no consequences at all, I don’t think it would grab me the same way. I might still double-check out of habit, because in my line of work you kind of build that into how you operate, but I don’t think I’d get stuck the way I do now.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again — breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took — and it’s like I can’t let myself trust that it’s done. A simple install that should be wrapped up in a reasonable amount of time ends up taking way longer because I keep circling back to the same things. And it’s not because I want it to look perfect or because there’s some exact order I need it in. It’s because in my head, if I missed something, it could matter.\n\nSo if there were truly no downside, no risk, no chance that a missed detail could hurt anything or cause a problem, then yeah, I think the urge would be a lot less. The checking is tied to that “what if” feeling. That’s why I’ll even look back through photos, or sometimes drive back to a site, because I’m trying to get that certainty that I didn’t leave something wrong. And lately it’s gotten to where people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly would decrease if you were certain that your work was safe, regardless of whether it was perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would decrease a lot if I could actually be certain it was safe. That’s really the thing I’m chasing, I guess — not perfection, but that feeling of, “Okay, this isn’t dangerous, I didn’t miss something that matters.”\n\nBecause what brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then the photos I took, and even after all that I can still get this nagging doubt that maybe I missed something. And sometimes I’ll be halfway home and still thinking about it, and I’ve actually driven back to a site just to look one more time.\n\nSo if someone could somehow guarantee, “It’s safe, even if it’s not flawless,” I think I’d be able to let go much easier. The problem is I can’t seem to get to that level of certainty on my own. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should because I keep looping back through the same checks, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious about your work if someone else double-checked it for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would help some, at least in the moment. If another electrician or someone I trusted looked it over and said, “No, this is right, the breaker matches, the labels are correct, the torque marks are there,” I’d probably feel my anxiety drop for a while.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely stop me, because part of the problem is I keep going back over it myself anyway. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and even after that I can still get this little doubt in my head like, “What if we both missed it?” or “What if I didn’t explain what I was worried about clearly enough?” So the reassurance helps, but it doesn’t always stick.\n\nAnd that’s kind of what’s been making work hard lately. I’m not just doing a reasonable double-check and moving on. I’m going back again and again, sometimes even after I’ve left the site. I’ve driven back before just to look at something one more time, even though by then I’ve already checked it, taken photos, and gone through it in my head. Then a simple install that should’ve been done in a normal amount of time stretches out way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check things over and over if you were absolutely certain that making a small mistake would have no significant consequences?\n\nPatient: If I was absolutely certain there were no real consequences, I think the checking would be way less. I mean, I might still glance over things because that’s how I’m used to working, but I don’t think I’d get trapped in it the same way.\n\nWhat keeps happening is I go back over the same job again and again — breakers, labels, torque marks, then I’ll pull up the photos I took and zoom in on them, and sometimes even after I’ve left I’ll start thinking, “Did I really see that right?” I’ve driven back to a site before just to check something that I had already checked. And then a simple install that should’ve been straightforward ends up taking way longer than it should.\n\nSo, yeah, if I truly believed a small mistake wouldn’t matter or wouldn’t cause any significant problem, I don’t think I’d feel the same pressure to keep rechecking. The reason I get stuck is that I can’t shake the feeling that if I missed one detail, it could turn into something important. People are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up, and that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work primarily driven by a fear of something going wrong if you don't check, rather than a desire to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s much more about something going wrong than about everything being perfect. I mean, I don’t really care if the work is beautiful or flawless in some extra way. It’s more like… if I don’t check the breaker one more time, or the label, or the torque mark, or the photo I took, there’s this feeling that I might have missed something important.\n\nThat’s what brings me in, really. I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look through the photos on my phone, and sometimes I still can’t let it go. I’ve even driven back to a site after leaving because I couldn’t stop thinking, “What if I missed it?”\n\nAnd then what should be a simple install just stretches out way longer than it should. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because people are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck. So no, it’s not really perfection I’m chasing — it’s trying to make sure nothing bad happens because I failed to catch something.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly at work driven more by the fear of making a mistake or by the desire to ensure everything is done perfectly?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more the fear of making a mistake. I mean, I want the work done right, obviously, but it’s not like I’m standing there trying to make it perfect in some artistic way or anything. It’s more that I get this nagging feeling that if I don’t check it again, I might miss something that matters.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. So, yeah, it’s really fear of missing something, not just wanting it to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking at work is more about avoiding the consequences of a potential mistake or about being in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding the consequences, I think. I’ll check the breakers, then I’ll check the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took and zoom in on them like I’m trying to prove to myself I saw it right. And sometimes even after all that, after I’ve packed up or even left, it still doesn’t feel done in my head. I’ve driven back to a site before because I couldn’t shake it.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess being in control is part of it, but not in the sense of wanting to run everything. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent that one missed detail from turning into a problem. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice me getting hung up, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work something you feel compelled to do because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or is it more about feeling in control of the job?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel compelled because I’m afraid something will go wrong if I don’t. And then a simple install that should’ve taken a normal amount of time ends up taking way longer than it should. It’s gotten to the point where people are starting to notice, which is embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s really fear of missing something, not just wanting it to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking at work is more about avoiding the consequences of a potential mistake or about being in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding the consequences, I think. I’ve driven back to a site before because I couldn’t shake it.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess being in control is part of it, but not in the sense of wanting to run everything. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent that one missed detail from turning into a problem. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice me getting hung up, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work something you feel compelled to do because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or is it more about feeling in control of the job?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel compelled because I’m afraid something will go wrong if I don’t. I mean, feeling in control is part of it in the sense that I want to leave a job knowing it’s safe and done right, but the thing that really drives it is that fear that I missed something.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s right. It’s much more about being afraid of what could happen if I made a mistake than about everything being perfect just for the sake of it.

What brings me in is that I keep going back over the same job again and again. Like, I’ll check the breaker, then I’ll check the label, then I’ll look at the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes zooming in or comparing things, and then I’ll still have this feeling like, “Wait, did I actually confirm that, or did I just think I did?” And then I’m back at it again.

It’s not that I’m trying to make the work look perfect or obsessing over neatness or anything like that. It’s more this fear that if I leave and there’s one thing wrong, one thing I missed, then there could be consequences. So I check until I feel like I can leave, but the problem is that feeling doesn’t come easily anymore.

Sometimes I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go in my head. And then what should have been a straightforward install ends up taking way longer than it should. People are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck on things, and that’s honestly embarrassing.

I mean, I want the work done right, obviously, but it’s not like I’m standing there trying to make it perfect in some artistic way or anything. It’s more that I get this nagging feeling that if I don’t check it again, I might miss something that matters.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breaker, then the label, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and then sometimes I’ll still think, “Did I really see that correctly?” And I know I’ve already checked it, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go. And then a simple install that should’ve taken a normal amount of time ends up taking way longer than it should. It’s gotten to the point where people are starting to notice, which is embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s really fear of missing something, not just wanting it to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking at work is more about avoiding the consequences of a potential mistake or about being in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding the consequences, I think. I mean, I do like to feel in control of the job, sure, because it’s electrical work and you’re supposed to be careful, but the thing that really hooks me is the thought that if I don’t check one more time, something could be wrong and I’ll have missed it.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over the same things again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then I’ll check the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took and zoom in on them like I’m trying to prove to myself I saw it right. And sometimes even after all that, after I’ve packed up or even left, it still doesn’t feel done in my head. I’ve driven back to a site before because I couldn’t shake it.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess being in control is part of it, but not in the sense of wanting to run everything. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent that one missed detail from turning into a problem. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice me getting hung up, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work something you feel compelled to do because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or is it more about feeling in control of the job?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel compelled because I’m afraid something will go wrong if I don’t. I mean, feeling in control is part of it in the sense that I want to leave a job knowing it’s safe and done right, but the thing that really drives it is that fear that I missed something.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breaker, then the label, then the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes more than once. And even then it can still feel like, “Did I actually verify that, or am I just remembering that I checked it?” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to be absolutely sure.\n\nSometimes I’ll already be packed up, or I’ll have left, and I still can’t shake it, and I’ve driven back to a site before just to look again. Then what should have been a pretty straightforward install ends up taking way longer than it should. And now people are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck on these checks, which is embarrassing. So yeah, it’s not really about control for its own sake — it’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening because I didn’t check enough.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work driven by a fear of the consequences of making a mistake, rather than a desire for everything to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s right. It’s much more about being afraid of what could happen if I made a mistake than about everything being perfect just for the sake of it.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over the same job again and again. Like, I’ll check the breaker, then I’ll check the label, then I’ll look at the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes zooming in or comparing things, and then I’ll still have this feeling like, “Wait, did I actually confirm that, or did I just think I did?” And then I’m back at it again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to make the work look perfect or obsessing over neatness or anything like that. It’s more this fear that if I leave and there’s one thing wrong, one thing I missed, then there could be consequences. So I check until I feel like I can leave, but the problem is that feeling doesn’t come easily anymore.\n\nSometimes I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go in my head. And then what should have been a straightforward install ends up taking way longer than it should. I mean, I do like to feel in control of the job, sure, because it’s electrical work and you’re supposed to be careful, but the thing that really hooks me is the thought that if I don’t check one more time, something could be wrong and I’ll have missed it.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over the same things again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then I’ll check the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took and zoom in on them like I’m trying to prove to myself I saw it right. So yeah, it’s not really about control for its own sake — it’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening because I didn’t check enough.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work driven by a fear of the consequences of making a mistake, rather than a desire for everything to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s right. It’s much more about being afraid of what could happen if I made a mistake than about everything being perfect just for the sake of it.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over the same job again and again. Like, I’ll check the breaker, then I’ll check the label, then I’ll look at the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes zooming in or comparing things, and then I’ll still have this feeling like, “Wait, did I actually confirm that, or did I just think I did?” And then I’m back at it again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to make the work look perfect or obsessing over neatness or anything like that. It’s more this fear that if I leave and there’s one thing wrong, one thing I missed, then there could be consequences. So I check until I feel like I can leave, but the problem is that feeling doesn’t come easily anymore.\n\nSometimes I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the fear of what could happen if something is wrong. I mean, I do want to feel like I’ve got the job handled, but that’s not really the thing driving it. It’s not like I’m standing there thinking, “I need to be in charge of every detail.” It’s more like, “If I miss this one thing, something bad could happen, and it’ll be on me.”

What brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll pull up the photos I took and look through those too. Sometimes I’ll zoom in on a picture like I’m trying to reassure myself that I really saw what I thought I saw. And then even after that, I’ll get this doubt like, “Did I actually check it properly, or am I just remembering that I meant to?”

There have been times where I’ve already left a site and I just can’t settle it in my head, so I’ve driven back to look again. And that’s when it starts getting ridiculous, because a simple install that should be done in a normal amount of time ends up taking way longer. People are starting to notice that I get stuck on these checks, and that’s embarrassing. So yeah, it’s definitely more fear of the consequences than just wanting control.

It’s not just, like, “I want it neat” or “I want the label straight.” It’s more that even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect—like I can remember checking it—this thought barges in that maybe I missed one loose neutral, or maybe I left something just slightly wrong that could heat up and start a fire.\n\nAnd I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you checked it, you know what you’re doing,” but it doesn’t really land. The thought just keeps following me. I can be halfway home and suddenly I’m picturing it, like, what if something’s sitting there loose behind the cover and nobody knows until it causes damage. So then I start replaying the whole job in my head, step by step, and it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tension or anxiety when you're having these repetitive thoughts about your work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. It’s more that I get kind of keyed up and stuck on it, and I’ll feel like I can’t relax until I’ve checked it again or gone back through it in my head. But as far as, like, noticing tension in one place or anything like that… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or have a certain symmetry to them for you to consider them complete?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that way, at least. Like, I’m not usually looking for things to be symmetrical or arranged a certain way before I can move on.\n\nWith work, there are obviously steps that have to happen in a certain order because that’s just how the job is done safely, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I’m not sitting there thinking, “This has to be perfectly even,” or “I need to do this sequence exactly or it feels wrong.” It’s more practical than that, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed myself needing symmetry in my house or with objects or anything like that. That’s the part that’s really messing with me, because people are starting to notice that I’m taking too long or getting hung up on stuff. So… yeah, I guess it doesn’t feel complete unless it feels completely right, and I have a hard time trusting that it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to control something when you're checking things over and over at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know if I would’ve used the word “control” at first, but that’s kind of what it feels like. Like I’m trying to get control over the possibility that I missed something.\n\nAt work, it’s mostly that I keep going back over the same things again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and then sometimes I’ll start thinking, “Wait, did I actually check that, or am I just remembering checking it?” And then I’m back looking again. Even when the job is basically done and nothing looks wrong, I still feel like I can’t quite let it be finished.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick normal check anymore. A simple install can take way longer than it should because I keep circling back over it. I’ve even driven back to a site sometimes because I couldn’t shake the feeling that I needed to verify something one more time. So yeah, I guess I’m trying to control that uncertainty, or control the risk that something was left wrong. But it gets to where the checking is taking over, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of making a mistake that's driving you to check things over and over, or is it more about making sure everything is done to a high standard?\n\nPatient: It’s more the mistake part, I think. I mean, I do care about doing good work — I’ve always taken pride in that — but the thing that gets stuck in my head isn’t, “Is this up to some perfect standard?” It’s more like, “What if I missed something dangerous?”\n\nLike even after I know I checked a panel or a disconnect, this thought will just barge in on me that maybe there’s one loose neutral I didn’t catch, or maybe I left something in a way that could start a fire. And I can try to tell myself, “No, you checked it, you looked at it, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really settle. It follows me. I’ll be driving home and replaying it in my head, trying to remember exactly what I saw or what my hand did, and then I start doubting whether I actually verified it or just think I did.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not really about making it beautiful or above-and-beyond perfect. It’s the possibility that I made one mistake and that one mistake could matter. It’s the consequences. I’ll tell myself, “You checked it, you know you checked it,” but then my brain starts picking at it, like, “Are you sure? I’ll be driving and still thinking about that one thing, replaying it, trying to convince myself I didn’t miss anything dangerous.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s not really about perfect. It’s fear that one mistake could have serious consequences, and I can’t seem to let that possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check at work need to be in a specific order or have a certain exactness to them for you to feel okay about moving on?\n\nPatient: It’s not so much that it has to be in one exact order, like a ritualized sequence or anything. And then instead of moving on, I’ll start over in my head, like, “Okay, but did I actually verify that breaker? Did I match that label right? Did I really see the torque mark, or am I just assuming I did?” And then I’m back there checking again.\n\nSometimes it turns into this loop where I can’t leave it alone. But lately that point takes a long time to get to, and people are starting to notice I’m getting stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check things over and over if there were no potential consequences for making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Honestly, if there were really no consequences at all, I don’t think it would grab me the same way. I might still double-check out of habit, because in my line of work you kind of build that into how you operate, but I don’t think I’d get stuck the way I do now.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again — breakers, labels, torque marks, the photos I took — and it’s like I can’t let myself trust that it’s done. A simple install that should be wrapped up in a reasonable amount of time ends up taking way longer because I keep circling back to the same things. And it’s not because I want it to look perfect or because there’s some exact order I need it in. It’s because in my head, if I missed something, it could matter.\n\nSo if there were truly no downside, no risk, no chance that a missed detail could hurt anything or cause a problem, then yeah, I think the urge would be a lot less. The checking is tied to that “what if” feeling. That’s why I’ll even look back through photos, or sometimes drive back to a site, because I’m trying to get that certainty that I didn’t leave something wrong. And lately it’s gotten to where people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly would decrease if you were certain that your work was safe, regardless of whether it was perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would decrease a lot if I could actually be certain it was safe. That’s really the thing I’m chasing, I guess — not perfection, but that feeling of, “Okay, this isn’t dangerous, I didn’t miss something that matters.”\n\nBecause what brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then the photos I took, and even after all that I can still get this nagging doubt that maybe I missed something. And sometimes I’ll be halfway home and still thinking about it, and I’ve actually driven back to a site just to look one more time.\n\nSo if someone could somehow guarantee, “It’s safe, even if it’s not flawless,” I think I’d be able to let go much easier. The problem is I can’t seem to get to that level of certainty on my own. A simple install ends up taking way longer than it should because I keep looping back through the same checks, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious about your work if someone else double-checked it for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would help some, at least in the moment. If another electrician or someone I trusted looked it over and said, “No, this is right, the breaker matches, the labels are correct, the torque marks are there,” I’d probably feel my anxiety drop for a while.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would completely stop me, because part of the problem is I keep going back over it myself anyway. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and even after that I can still get this little doubt in my head like, “What if we both missed it?” or “What if I didn’t explain what I was worried about clearly enough?” So the reassurance helps, but it doesn’t always stick.\n\nAnd that’s kind of what’s been making work hard lately. I’m not just doing a reasonable double-check and moving on. I’m going back again and again, sometimes even after I’ve left the site. I’ve driven back before just to look at something one more time, even though by then I’ve already checked it, taken photos, and gone through it in my head. Then a simple install that should’ve been done in a normal amount of time stretches out way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check things over and over if you were absolutely certain that making a small mistake would have no significant consequences?\n\nPatient: If I was absolutely certain there were no real consequences, I think the checking would be way less. I mean, I might still glance over things because that’s how I’m used to working, but I don’t think I’d get trapped in it the same way.\n\nWhat keeps happening is I go back over the same job again and again — breakers, labels, torque marks, then I’ll pull up the photos I took and zoom in on them, and sometimes even after I’ve left I’ll start thinking, “Did I really see that right?” I’ve driven back to a site before just to check something that I had already checked. And then a simple install that should’ve been straightforward ends up taking way longer than it should.\n\nSo, yeah, if I truly believed a small mistake wouldn’t matter or wouldn’t cause any significant problem, I don’t think I’d feel the same pressure to keep rechecking. The reason I get stuck is that I can’t shake the feeling that if I missed one detail, it could turn into something important. People are starting to notice that I’m getting hung up, and that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work primarily driven by a fear of something going wrong if you don't check, rather than a desire to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s much more about something going wrong than about everything being perfect. I mean, I don’t really care if the work is beautiful or flawless in some extra way. It’s more like… if I don’t check the breaker one more time, or the label, or the torque mark, or the photo I took, there’s this feeling that I might have missed something important.\n\nThat’s what brings me in, really. So no, it’s not really perfection I’m chasing — it’s trying to make sure nothing bad happens because I failed to catch something.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly at work driven more by the fear of making a mistake or by the desire to ensure everything is done perfectly?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more the fear of making a mistake. I mean, I want the work done right, obviously, but it’s not like I’m standing there trying to make it perfect in some artistic way or anything. It’s more that I get this nagging feeling that if I don’t check it again, I might miss something that matters.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breaker, then the label, then the torque marks, then I’ll look at the photos I took, and then sometimes I’ll still think, “Did I really see that correctly?” And I know I’ve already checked it, but it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nThere have been times where I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go. And then a simple install that should’ve taken a normal amount of time ends up taking way longer than it should. It’s gotten to the point where people are starting to notice, which is embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s really fear of missing something, not just wanting it to be perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking at work is more about avoiding the consequences of a potential mistake or about being in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about avoiding the consequences, I think. And then a simple install takes way longer than it should, and people are starting to notice me getting hung up, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work something you feel compelled to do because you fear something will go wrong if you don't, or is it more about feeling in control of the job?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I feel compelled because I’m afraid something will go wrong if I don’t. I mean, feeling in control is part of it in the sense that I want to leave a job knowing it’s safe and done right, but the thing that really drives it is that fear that I missed something.\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breaker, then the label, then the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes more than once. And even then it can still feel like, “Did I actually verify that, or am I just remembering that I checked it?” It sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like I have to be absolutely sure.\n\nSometimes I’ll already be packed up, or I’ll have left, and I still can’t shake it, and I’ve driven back to a site before just to look again. Then what should have been a pretty straightforward install ends up taking way longer than it should. And now people are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck on these checks, which is embarrassing. So yeah, it’s not really about control for its own sake — it’s more like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening because I didn’t check enough.\n\nDoctor: Is your repetitive checking at work driven by a fear of the consequences of making a mistake, rather than a desire for everything to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s right. It’s much more about being afraid of what could happen if I made a mistake than about everything being perfect just for the sake of it.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep going back over the same job again and again. Like, I’ll check the breaker, then I’ll check the label, then I’ll look at the torque marks, then I’ll go back through the photos I took, sometimes zooming in or comparing things, and then I’ll still have this feeling like, “Wait, did I actually confirm that, or did I just think I did?” And then I’m back at it again.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to make the work look perfect or obsessing over neatness or anything like that. It’s more this fear that if I leave and there’s one thing wrong, one thing I missed, then there could be consequences. So I check until I feel like I can leave, but the problem is that feeling doesn’t come easily anymore.\n\nSometimes I’ve even driven back to a site because I couldn’t let it go in my head. And then what should have been a straightforward install ends up taking way longer than it should. People are starting to notice that I’m getting stuck on things, and that’s honestly embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to repeatedly check your work at the job site driven more by the fear of what could happen if something is wrong, or by the need to feel in control of the work?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear of what could happen if something is wrong. I mean, I do want to feel like I’ve got the job handled, but that’s not really the thing driving it. It’s not like I’m standing there thinking, “I need to be in charge of every detail.” It’s more like, “If I miss this one thing, something bad could happen, and it’ll be on me.”\n\nWhat brings me in is I keep going back over jobs again and again. I’ll check the breakers, then the labels, then the torque marks, then I’ll pull up the photos I took and look through those too. Sometimes I’ll zoom in on a picture like I’m trying to reassure myself that I really saw what I thought I saw. And then even after that, I’ll get this doubt like, “Did I actually check it properly, or am I just remembering that I meant to?”\n\nThere have been times where I’ve already left a site and I just can’t settle it in my head, so I’ve driven back to look again. And that’s when it starts getting ridiculous, because a simple install that should be done in a normal amount of time ends up taking way longer. People are starting to notice that I get stuck on these checks, and that’s embarrassing. So yeah, it’s definitely more fear of the consequences than just wanting control."
